--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This is the title page of a foundational work in German Idealism, establishing the dialogue format and the central theme of reconciling the divine with nature.</meta>

# Bruno
->or<-
->concerning<-
->the divine<-
->and<-
->natural<-
## Principle of Things.

---

## A Dialogue.
->Edited<-
->by<-
### Schelling. <note>Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph Schelling (1775–1854) was a major philosopher of German Idealism. This work is named after Giordano Bruno, the Renaissance thinker who argued for an infinite universe and the unity of God and nature.</note>

---

->Berlin.<-
->Published by Johann Friedrich Unger.<-
->1 8 0 2.<-

<summary>The title page of Schelling's 1802 philosophical dialogue, which explores the fundamental relationship between divinity and the physical world.</summary>
<keywords>Schelling, Giordano Bruno, philosophy of nature, German Idealism, dialogue, metaphysics, 19th-century philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This is the title page of the manuscript, serving as the introduction to the work.</meta>

# B r u n o
<note>The title refers to Giordano Bruno (1548–1600), the Italian Dominican friar and philosopher. Schelling admired Bruno's "heroic" spirit and his pantheistic view that God and the universe are one. In this text, "Bruno" acts as a character representing Schelling’s own philosophical positions.</note>

### or

## on the Divine
<term>Divine Principle (göttliches Princip): The ultimate, spiritual source or "first cause" of everything that exists. In this context, it refers to the absolute foundation of reality.</term>

### and

## Natural Principle of Things.
<term>Natural Principle (natürliches Princip): The fundamental law or force governing the physical world. Schelling’s goal was to show that this natural force and the "Divine Principle" are ultimately the same.</term>

---

## A D i a l o g u e .
<note>original: "Ein Gespräch"</note>
<note>Following the tradition of Plato, Schelling chooses the "dialogue" form to present his ideas through a conversation between characters, allowing him to explore different viewpoints and reach a synthesis of "Identity" between nature and spirit.</note>

<summary>This is the title page of Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph Schelling's "Bruno," a key work of German Idealism that explores the essential unity between the spiritual (divine) and the material (natural) worlds. It establishes the "Philosophy of Identity," which suggests that God and Nature are two sides of the same absolute reality.</summary>

<keywords>Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph Schelling, Giordano Bruno, Divine Principle, Natural Principle, Philosophy of Identity, Dialogue, Naturphilosophie, German Idealism</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>The text on this page is extremely faded, appearing as a faint proof print or "ghost text," marking the formal beginning of the manuscript's title section.</meta>

# G R O U N D
<term>Ground (Grund): In the context of German Idealism, "Ground" refers to the fundamental basis or underlying foundation of all existence—the "why" behind the "is."</term>

->or<-

## On the Divine
<term>Divine Principle (göttliches Princip): The ultimate, spiritual source or absolute foundation of reality. Schelling seeks to understand this not just as a religious concept, but as a philosophical necessity.</term>

->and<-

## Natural Principle of Things.
<term>Natural Principle (natürliches Princip): The fundamental law or internal force that governs the physical world. A central goal of this work is to prove that the "Natural" and the "Divine" are essentially one and the same.</term>

---

### An Attempt.
<note>original: "Ein Versuch." This was a common subtitle for philosophical treatises of the era, indicating a formal inquiry or essay that tests a specific hypothesis or system of thought.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for a philosophical treatise exploring the "Ground" of reality. It introduces the author's investigation into the unified origin of both the spiritual (divine) and material (natural) worlds.</summary>

<keywords>Ground, Divine Principle, Natural Principle, Philosophy of Nature, German Idealism, Metaphysics, Ontology</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the formal dialogue, transitioning from the metaphysical title sections to a conversation between three figures—Anselmo, Lucian, and Alexander—concerning the intersection of aesthetics and philosophy.</meta>

### Anselmo

Will you repeat for us, O Lucian, what you asserted yesterday—while we were speaking about the arrangement of the **mysteries**—concerning truth and beauty? <note>original: "Mysterien." In the 18th and 19th centuries, the "Mysteries" often referred to the secret religious rites of Ancient Greece, which philosophers used as a metaphor for the hidden truths of nature and art.</note>

**Lucian.** My opinion was that the highest truth could exist in many works without the prize of beauty necessarily being awarded to them for that reason. <note>original: "Meynung." This archaic spelling of "Meinung" (opinion) is common in texts from the late 1700s.</note>

**Anselmo.** But you, Alexander, declared on the contrary that truth alone fulfills all the requirements of **art**, and that it is only through truth that a work becomes truly beautiful.

**Alexander.** So I asserted.

**Anselmo.** Does it please you that we resume this conversation and now decide the dispute which remained undecided when time commanded our parting? For fortunately—

<summary>This page introduces a philosophical dialogue debating the relationship between truth and beauty in art. The characters Lucian and Alexander represent opposing views on whether a work of art requires more than just "truth" to be considered "beautiful."</summary>

<keywords>truth, beauty, aesthetics, art, mysteries, philosophical dialogue, Lucian, Alexander, Anselmo</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory conversation between Anselmo, Lucian, and Alexander, picking up immediately from Anselmo’s sentence about their fortunate reunion.</meta>

has reunited us here again, not by an explicit agreement, to be sure, but through a secret accord.

**Lucian.** Welcome is every wave of conversation that leads us back into the current of discourse.

**Alexander.** The competition of shared discourse penetrates ever deeper into the core of the matter; beginning softly, progressing slowly, it finally swells deeply, sweeping the participants along and filling everyone with delight.

**Anselmo.** Did the origin of our dispute not lie in what we had established concerning the **mysteries** and **mythology**, as well as the relationship between **philosophers** and **poets**? <note>original: "Mysterien und die Mythologie." The dialogue situates itself in the late 18th-century tradition of "Aesthetic Education," where the relationship between the analytical mind (the philosopher) and the creative spirit (the poet) was a central debate.</note>

**Lucian.** So it was.

**Anselmo.** Does it not seem good to you that, while we settle this dispute, the discourse should simultaneously return to its origin, so that afterward we may continue to build undisturbed upon the secure foundation we have laid?

**Alexander.** Excellent.

**Anselmo.** You then, Lucian—since it seems possible to you that a work, without being beautiful, could achieve the perfection of the highest truth— <note>The sentence ends abruptly here as the page concludes; the argument likely continues by asking Lucian to justify how "truth" can exist independently of "beauty."</note>

<note>Vocabulary: Lucian, Alexander, Anselmo, Mysteries, Mythology, Philosophers, Poets, Truth</note>

<summary>The three participants agree to revisit the roots of their debate regarding the relationship between philosophers and poets. Anselmo prepares to challenge Lucian’s earlier claim that a work can possess "highest truth" without being "beautiful."</summary>

<keywords>aesthetics, philosophy, poetry, mysteries, mythology, truth, beauty, dialogue, Lucian, Alexander, Anselmo</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Anselmo's address to Lucian and Alexander, completing the sentence started on the previous page regarding the nature of truth and beauty.</meta>

...could have, you seem to call something **truth** which we **philosophers** might perhaps not even grant that name. You, however, Alexander, by allowing a work to be beautiful only through its **truth**, doubt that there could be a point where both—equally absolute, neither dependent on nor subordinate to the other, each being the highest in its own right—are so entirely one and the same that one could be substituted for the other, and the work expressing that point could be viewed equally through either quality. <note>Anselmo is proposing a "point of indifference" or identity where Truth and Beauty become indistinguishable. This reflects late 18th-century German Idealism, which sought to unite the objective (truth) with the subjective/sensory (beauty).</note> Do you not then think it necessary that we first seek to agree upon what should be called **truth**, and then what should be called **beauty**? This way, we avoid equating something merely secondary with beauty; or, by setting a "truth" that is not inherently true as incomparable to beauty, we avoid losing sight of that which is the only genuine **truth**.

**Lucian.** A worthy subject and matter for our conversation.

<summary>Anselmo proposes that the group must define "truth" and "beauty" before continuing, suggesting that in the greatest works of art, these two concepts are identical and interchangeable. Lucian agrees to make this the focus of their discourse.</summary>

<keywords>truth, beauty, philosophy, aesthetics, Alexander, Lucian, definition of terms, identity of opposites</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Anselmo, Alexander, and Lucian. Following the previous page’s proposal to define their terms, Anselmo now addresses Alexander specifically to begin establishing a philosophical definition of "Truth" that distinguishes it from mere temporary certainty or sensory perception.</meta>

**Anselmo.** But if you are satisfied with this, O excellent one—you who have awarded the prize to **Truth** over **Beauty**, unconcerned that she counts but few who can endure her stern countenance or the sight of the **Aegis** <note>original: "Ägide". In Greek mythology, the Aegis was the shield of Zeus or Athena. Anselmo uses it here as a metaphor for the daunting, perhaps even terrifying, power of absolute Truth.</note>—then I turn to you.

**Alexander.** Most gladly do I follow you, O friend, to reach an understanding regarding the idea of **Truth**.

**Anselmo.** Setting **Truth**, therefore, above everything and even above **Beauty**, O friend, you will be all the less able to hesitate in further attributing to her the highest qualities, and you will not allow this venerable name to be applied haphazardly to everything that is commonly understood by it.

**Alexander.** Certainly.

**Anselmo.** You will accordingly not grant the quality of **Truth** to any **knowledge** <term>Erkenntniß: Often translated as "cognition" or "knowledge," referring here to a formal or systematic grasp of a subject.</term> which carries with it only a present or generally transient certainty.

**Alexander.** By no means shall I.

**Anselmo.** For this reason, you will never [grant it] to such **knowledge** which [is produced] only through the immediate **affections** <term>Affectionen: In the philosophical tradition of the time (e.g., Kant), this refers to the way the mind is affected by sensory impressions or external stimuli.</term> of the...

<summary>Anselmo begins a formal inquiry into the nature of Truth, gaining Alexander's agreement that "Truth" should only be applied to that which is eternal and certain, rather than transient sensory impressions.</summary>

<keywords>Anselmo, Alexander, Truth, Beauty, Aegis, certainty, knowledge, affections, philosophical dialogue</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 6. Anselmo is concluding his question regarding whether Alexander would attribute "Truth" to knowledge derived from the physical body's sensations. The text transitions from the previous page's unfinished sentence: "affections of the [body]..."</meta>

body, or refers immediately only to them, to ascribe **Truth**.

**Alexander.** Impossible, for I know that these, together with the object that undergoes them, are subject to the conditions of **time**.

**Anselmo.** For the same reason, you will grant **Truth** to no **knowledge** <term>Erkenntniß: refers to cognition or the systematic grasp of an object by the mind.</term> that is confused, indistinct, or inadequate to the thing as it is in itself.

**Alexander.** None; for every such knowledge is merely of a **sensory** nature and mediated through **affections** <term>Affectionen: the way the mind is affected by external stimuli or bodily impressions.</term>.

**Anselmo.** Would you, furthermore, designate with the sublime name of **Truth** that which possesses a generally lasting but nonetheless subordinate **certainty** <term>Gewißheit: a state of being sure; here Anselmo distinguishes between a subjective feeling of being sure and the objective, absolute Truth.</term>, such that it would have validity only for the human perspective, or any other perspective that is not the highest?

**Alexander.** Not this either, if such a thing existed.

**Anselmo.** You doubt whether such a thing exists. Let us then see what you would contrast with that which we have called **transient**, or in what you place **imperishable certainty**.

<summary>In this passage, Anselmo and Alexander establish that "Truth" cannot be found in sensory impressions or temporal conditions. They conclude that true knowledge must be more than just a human perspective; it must possess an "imperishable certainty" that transcends the physical body.</summary>

<keywords>Truth, cognition, certainty, time, sensory perception, affections, transient vs. imperishable, human perspective, Anselmo, Alexander</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 7, where Anselmo challenged Alexander to define where he places "imperishable certainty." Alexander begins this page by answering that question.</meta>

**Alexander.** Necessarily in that **truth**, which holds valid not only for individual things but for all, and not only for a specific time but for all time.

**Anselmo.** Would you really place **imperishable certainty** in that which, though valid for all time, nevertheless holds validity only in relation to **time**? Is it not obvious that the truth which applies to time and things within time is "imperishable" only in relation to that which itself is not eternal—thus, not considered absolutely and in itself? <note>Anselmo is making a sophisticated distinction here: something might be true "forever" within the universe, but if the universe itself is finite, that truth is still only "relative" rather than "absolute."</note> It is, however, unthinkable that what applies only to the **finite** <term>Endlichen: refers to things that have limits, boundaries, or an end, as opposed to the infinite.</term>—even if it applies universally to it—could have a higher value than the finite itself, or that we could grant it anything more than a **relative truth**, since it stands and falls together with the finite. For what human would deny that every **effect** is preceded by its **cause**, and that this **certainty**—without even being tested against physical objects—is beyond doubt, through the mere relationship of **finite cognition** <term>Erkennens: the mental process of knowing or perceiving. Anselmo is discussing "a priori" knowledge—things we know are true without needing to see them happen.</term> to the very concept of knowing? But if this same principle, beyond...

<summary>Anselmo argues that truths governing finite things (like the law of cause and effect) are only relatively certain because they are bound to the concept of time and the finite mind. He challenges Alexander to look for a higher, absolute truth that exists independently of the temporal world.</summary>

<keywords>truth, certainty, time, the finite, cause and effect, cognition, relative truth, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 8. Anselmo is in the middle of a sentence arguing that principles governing the finite world (like causality) lose their validity if separated from the finite context.</meta>

...relation to that which is finite in itself has no meaning, then it is also impossible that truth should belong to it. For have you not agreed with me that what possesses certainty only for a subordinate way of looking at things <note>By "subordinate way of looking at things," Anselmo refers to a limited, human perspective rather than a divine or absolute one.</note> cannot be considered true in the genuine sense?

*Alexander.* Certainly.

*Anselmo.* Furthermore, you will not be able to deny that the knowledge of the **finite** <term>Endliche: things that are limited in space, time, or nature.</term> and the **temporal** <term>Zeitliche: things existing within the flow of time.</term>, as such, only takes place within finite **cognition** <term>Erkennen: the act of knowing or perceiving.</term> itself, but not in the **absolute**. Would you be content with a truth that exists merely for the cognition of finite beings and not absolutely—a truth that is not also truth in the eyes of God and the highest cognition? Or does not all our striving aim to know things as they are prefigured in that **archetypal intellect** <term>urbildliche Verstande: a philosophical concept, often associated with Kant or Plato, referring to a divine mind that perceives things as they truly are, rather than through filtered human senses.</term>, of which we perceive only mere **reflections** <term>Abbilder: copies or mirror images; the idea that human knowledge is just a shadow of a higher reality.</term> in our own?

*Alexander.* It is difficult to deny.

*Anselmo.* But this highest cognition—can you conceive of it at all under the conditions of time?

<summary>Anselmo argues that true knowledge must transcend the human, finite perspective and strive for the "archetypal" truth as seen by a divine or absolute intellect outside of time.</summary>
<keywords>finite, temporal, absolute, cognition, archetypal intellect, reflections, Anselmo, Alexander</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 9. Anselmo is challenging Alexander to consider whether a "highest cognition" (a divine or absolute way of knowing) can be subject to the same rules as human thought, specifically focusing on the concepts of time and the finite.</meta>

*Alexander.* Impossible.

*Anselmo.* Or even [can you conceive of it] as **determined** <note>In this philosophical context, "determined" means to be limited, defined, or given specific boundaries by something else.</note> by **concepts** <term>Begriffe: the mental categories or logical rules—like cause and effect—that the mind uses to organize experiences.</term> which, although universal and infinite in themselves, nevertheless relate only to time and the **finite**?

*Alexander.* Not as determined by such concepts, certainly, but rather as **determining** these concepts.

*Anselmo.* This amounts to the same thing for us here; for in our **finite cognition** <term>endlichen Erkennen: the human way of knowing, which is limited by our senses and our placement in time.</term>, we do not appear to ourselves as the ones determining those concepts, but rather as being determined by them. And if we are the ones determining them, it is evidently through a higher form of knowing.

We must therefore, in any case, accept it as an established principle that no **absolute truth** <note>"Absolute truth" refers to a reality that is true regardless of human perspective, time, or location—truth as it exists in the mind of the infinite.</note> belongs to that knowledge which relates at all to time or the temporal existence of things. This holds even if we assume that this knowledge does not itself arise within time, and even if it applies to all of infinite time as well as to all things within time. It still lacks absolute truth because it presupposes a higher cognition. This higher cognition is of such a nature that it is independent of all time and has no reference to time; it exists in itself and is, therefore, simply **eternal**.

<summary>Anselmo argues that any knowledge related to time or finite things cannot be "absolute truth," because absolute truth requires a higher, eternal form of cognition that exists entirely outside of time.</summary>

<keywords>absolute truth, eternity, finite cognition, temporal existence, determining concepts, philosophy of time</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 9 and 10. Anselmo and Alexander are refining their definition of truth, moving away from human, time-bound perception toward an "eternal" perspective. Anselmo has just argued that absolute truth cannot exist within the realm of time.</meta>

*Alexander.* This conclusion is unavoidable based on our first premises.

*Anselmo.* We will therefore only have reached the peak of truth itself, and be able to perceive and represent things with truth, once our thoughts have attained the **timeless existence** <term>unzeitlichen Daseyn: a state of being that is not subject to the flow of past, present, and future; existence as it is in itself, regardless of time.</term> of things and their **eternal concepts** <term>ewigen Begriffen: the unchanging, universal ideas or "blueprints" of things that exist in the mind of the infinite.</term>.

*Alexander.* I cannot deny it, though you have not yet shown how we might reach that state.

*Anselmo.* That question does not concern us here, for we are focused solely on the idea of truth. We would consider it unworthy to lower the standard of truth, or to pull it down from its height, simply to make it easier for most people to reach. <note>Anselmo is arguing for an "elitist" or rigorous definition of truth; he believes truth should not be redefined just to make it more accessible to the average person.</note> — But does it please you that we proceed with our investigations in this manner?

*Alexander.* Certainly.

*Anselmo.* Then let us further consider the difference between **eternal** and **temporal knowing** <term>zeitlichen Erkennens: the human way of understanding things through a sequence of moments, which Anselmo views as inherently limited compared to a "timeless" understanding.</term>. Do you hold it possible that everything we call erroneous, perverse, imperfect, and so on, actually [exists] in...

<summary>Anselmo and Alexander agree that true knowledge requires reaching a "timeless" perspective of eternal concepts, refusing to lower the definition of truth for the sake of accessibility. They begin to discuss whether human concepts like "imperfection" or "error" have any reality in an eternal context.</summary>

<keywords>absolute truth, timeless existence, eternal concepts, temporal knowing, perfection, philosophical dialogue</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue from page 11. Anselmo is leading Alexander to consider whether human concepts of "error" and "imperfection" are objective realities or merely subjective judgments based on our limited perspective.</meta>

[...itself,] or whether it is such rather only in regard to our **way of looking at it**? <term>Betrachtungsweise: a specific mode of observation or perspective; Anselmo is suggesting that what we perceive as "flawed" might only appear so because we lack the full context of the universe.</term>

*Alexander.* I cannot imagine that, for example, the **imperfection** <term>Unvollkommenheit: the state of being incomplete or failing to meet a standard of excellence.</term> of any human work does not actually exist in regard to that work; nor that what we necessarily think of as **erroneous** <term>irrig: containing a mistake or "irrthum."</term> is not also actually false.

*Anselmo.* Do not let the meaning of the question escape you, oh friend. I am not speaking of what the work is when considered individually, separated from the whole. That one person produces something entirely **distorted** <term>Verkehrtes: literally "turned the wrong way"; something perverse, contrary to order, or nonsensical.</term> instead of a perfect work, or that another produces nothing but false propositions instead of true ones, is—when viewed truly—neither distortion nor error. Rather, if that person, being constituted exactly as he is, could produce something perfect or anything other than the nonsensical and foolish, *that* would instead be called an error and a real **distortion of nature** <note>Anselmo is presenting a deterministic argument: everything in nature acts according to its design. If a "foolish" person acted "wisely," it would be a violation of their nature, which is impossible.</note>—both of which are impossible. Since no one produces anything other than what arises partly from the **particularity of his nature** <term>Eigenthümlichkeit seiner Natur: the unique, inherent characteristics and limitations that define an individual being.</term>, and partly from the influences which [act] upon him from without... <note>The German text ends mid-word with "auf=" which likely completes as "außen" (without/outside).</note>

<summary>Anselmo challenges the idea of objective "error," arguing that every action and creation is a necessary and "natural" result of an individual's specific nature and external influences.</summary>
<keywords>imperfection, error, laws of nature, perspective, determinism, Alexander, Anselmo</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues Anselmo’s deterministic argument from page 12. He is explaining to Alexander that what we perceive as individual failure or "imperfection" is actually a necessary part of the universal order, as every action is the inevitable result of prior causes.</meta>

[...of those things that] have happened, it necessarily follows that each person—the one through his **error** <term>Irrthum: a mistake, delusion, or false belief.</term>, the other through the **imperfection** <term>Unvollkommenheit: the state of being incomplete or failing to meet a standard of excellence.</term> of his work—expresses the highest truth and the highest perfection of the whole. He confirms by his very example that no lie is possible in nature.

*Alexander.* You seem to be catching yourself in your own arguments. For that the error of the one should be truth, and the imperfection of the other should be perfection, follows indeed from the admitted **distortion** <term>Verkehrtheit: literally "turned the wrong way"; a state of being twisted, perverse, or contrary to the natural order.</term> of their nature—

*Anselmo.* Which, in turn, when considered in itself, is no distortion at all. For since, for example, the former was begotten by such a father, and the latter was determined by such external influences, their current **condition** <note>original: "Beschaffenheit"</note> is entirely according to the rule and is necessary within the general order of things.

*Alexander.* According to this view, you must only be careful not to admit a beginning of imperfection.

*Anselmo.* Certainly, just as it is generally impossible to admit a beginning of time—

<meta>This page continues Anselmo's dialogue regarding the two failures in art: the artist who clings to "raw naturalness" and the artist who creates empty forms. Anselmo is about to transition from the unity of beauty and truth to the unity of philosophy and poetry.</meta>

<margin>+</margin>
But, my friends, now that we have proven the supreme unity of beauty and truth, it seems to me that the unity of **philosophy** and **poetry** is also proven. For what does the former strive for, if not that very eternal truth which is one with beauty? And the latter, what does it seek if not that unborn and immortal beauty, which is one and the same with truth? Does it please you, my dear friend, that we explain this relationship further, so that we might return to our starting point?

**Alexander.** Indeed, I wish for this.

**Anselmo.** Thus, the highest beauty and truth of all things are perceived in one and the same **Idea**. <term>Idea (Idee): In this philosophical tradition, an "Idea" is not just a thought, but the eternal, perfect archetype or spiritual essence of a thing that exists beyond the physical world.</term>

<summary>Anselmo argues that because beauty and truth are identical at their highest level, the disciplines of philosophy and poetry are also unified in their pursuit of the "Idea." He contrasts this with "raw naturalness" and empty aestheticism.</summary>

<summary>Anselmo argues that great artists are often "possessed" by the idea of beauty rather than consciously controlling it, causing them to act as unlearned conduits for divine truths. <meta>This page continues the dialogue where Bruno critiques the idea that unity and difference are merely relatively opposed. He is pushing Lucian to see that if they are only relatively opposed, they must limit each other like physical objects, which contradicts the idea of an absolute unity.</meta>

**Lucian.** Certainly.

**Bruno.** Insofar as they are merely **relatively** opposed, they can only be **one** relatively, and they will mutually bound and restrict one another, just like the two bodies we assumed earlier.

**Lucian.** Necessarily.

**Bruno.** And only to the extent that they bound and mutually restrict one another will unity be **clouded** <note>German: *getrübt*. **Lucian.** Quite right.

**Bruno.** You therefore posit, by positing unity as "clouded," a necessary relationship of **cause and effect** between the two. **Lucian.** The gods forbid that I should take this seriously!

**Bruno.** You cannot, therefore, seriously assume that we [perceive] the unity, which...

<summary>Bruno argues that if unity and difference are only relatively opposed, they must limit each other like physical objects, leading to a "clouded" unity. He critiques the idea that unity and multiplicity exist in a simple cause-and-effect relationship, a view Lucian rejects.</summary>

Just reflect on what you have already thought, and tell me whether you can conceive of a more perfect unity than that between the **object** and its **image**, even though it is absolutely impossible that both ever come together in a third <note>The "third" refers to a medium or physical space where the actual object and its reflection would merge; Bruno argues their unity exists in a higher metaphysical realm rather than a physical one.</note>. Now, assume such a **destiny** <note>original: *Verhängniß*. In this philosophical context, it refers to a necessary structural arrangement or the "inner necessity" of the universe's design.</note> and this **order of the world**, that generally, when the object exists, so too does the image, and when the image exists, so too does the object; for that very reason and on this ground, both will necessarily be together everywhere, precisely because they are together **nowhere**. For that which is absolutely and infinitely opposed, can

<summary>Bruno uses the analogy of an object and its reflection to explain that absolute opposites are actually perfectly unified within a higher order. He argues that their constant coexistence is guaranteed precisely by the fact that they can never physically merge.</summary>
<keywords>absolute unity, absolute opposition, object and image, metaphysics, light and body, world order, coincidence of opposites</keywords>

...only be infinitely united. Everything that is stated about unity can only be stated in relation to that absolute. [ideality] we will be able to define as an **infinite thinking**, but oppose this with what you have called **intuition** <term>Intuition (*Anschauen*): In this philosophical context, it refers to a direct, immediate perception or "looking at" something, rather than a logical, step-by-step thought process.</term>.

[high]est unity of **thinking** and **intuition** <term>Intuition (*Anschauen*): In this context, it refers to a direct, immediate perception or "looking at" reality, rather than a step-by-step logical process.</term>, be expressed. eternal **Being** of things in their ideas, reconcile this infinite determination of things by one another, which seems to relate only to temporal **existence** <term>Existence (*Daseyn*): In German philosophy of this period, this often refers to specific, "here-and-now" existence in the physical world of time, as opposed to eternal or absolute Being.</term>?

**Bruno.** It is necessary, therefore, that if the infinite exists, the finite must also be present with it, inseparable from it, within that which we have established as eternal.

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Bruno and Lucian. Bruno is further developing the argument that Knowledge (Ideal) and Being (Real) are mutually dependent "relative unities," meaning neither can exist or be defined without the other.</meta>

**Lucian.** Correct.

**Bruno.** But neither of these two unities is anything **in itself** <note>original: *an sich*. This is a central concept in German philosophy referring to the essence of a thing as it exists independently of our senses or descriptions.</note>; for each exists only through the other.

**Lucian.** It seems so.

**Bruno.** It is obvious; for you can no more **posit** <note>As noted previously, to "posit" (*setzen*) is to establish a fact or concept through the movement of thought.</note> **knowledge** without immediately and simultaneously positing **being**, as you can posit **being** without immediately and simultaneously positing **knowledge**.

**Bruno.** Therefore, neither of these two unities can be the **principle** <term>Principle (*Princip*): In this context, the foundational source or "starting point" from which something else originates.</term> of the other.

**Lucian.** Neither.

**Bruno.** **Knowledge**, insofar as it is a relative unity, is as little the principle of **being**, as **being**, insofar as it is a relative unity, is the principle of **knowledge**.

**Lucian.** Agreed.

**Bruno.** You therefore cannot dissolve either of these **determinations** <term>Determinations (*Bestimmtheiten*): The specific characteristics or qualities that define a concept and distinguish it from others.</term> into the other; for the one stands and falls with the other, so that by taking one away, you also cancel the other.

**Lucian.** Certainly, and besides, that was not my **opinion**.

Thought and Being, ideal and real. In this **absolute unity**, however—since, as has been shown, everything within it is perfect and itself absolute—nothing is distinguishable from anything else. But because the finite, although in a **real** sense completely equal to the infinite, does not cease to be finite in an **ideal** sense, there exists within that unity once again the **difference** of all forms. This difference, however, remains within the unity itself, unseparated from **indifference** <term>**Indifference**: A philosophical state where opposites (such as subject and object, or essence and form) are perfectly balanced or identical, rendering them indistinguishable.</term>.

...does not fall into them themselves and become alive in them. Insofar as this is the case, however, they are independent, living, free, and even absolute like the **celestial bodies** <note>The author views planets and stars not just as rocks in space, but as autonomous, "living" entities that manifest the absolute law of the universe.</note>.

**Gravity**, meanwhile (for this is necessary to know beforehand), which unceasingly absorbs **difference** into the general **indifference** <term>original: *Indifferenz*. In this philosophical system, "indifference" refers to the point of absolute neutrality or identity where opposing forces are balanced and unified.</term>, is in itself indivisible; therefore, no matter how a sensory thing may be divided, gravity itself is not divided and is in itself neither increased nor decreased. Furthermore, being of such a nature that it is the indifference of **space** and **time**, it can be opposed to neither of them; it can neither decrease with increasing space (which is the expression of difference) nor increase with decreasing space. The more a thing separates itself from the **totality** <note>original: *Allheit*</note>, the less desire or striving there is in it—viewed ideally—to return to the unity of all; yet gravity does not change because of this and remains unmoved and equal toward all.

Therefore, what we require of every thing for its reality can be expressed through three levels or **powers** <term>original: *Potenzen*. <meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial mechanics from page 106, transitioning from the mathematical relationship of time and space to the metaphysical nature of the "revolution" (Umlauf) itself. Think, then, of the **revolution** <term>original: *Umlauf*. The orbital or cyclical motion of a celestial body.</term> itself as simply and entirely whole, simple, not as something composite, but as an absolute **unity** <term>original: *Einheit*.</term>. Within this unity, that through which a thing exists in unity—and which is commonly called **gravity** <term>original: *Schwere*. In this philosophical context, gravity represents the force of attraction that pulls an object toward a central point of oneness.</term>—and that through which it exists within itself—which is viewed as the opposite of gravity—are completely identical forms; both are the same whole. 

A thing <note>The author uses "A thing" (Ein Ding) here as a rhetorical anchor to emphasize the singularity of the object being discussed.</note>, for while existing in unity, a thing cannot be remote from it within itself, nor can it exist within itself while being in unity, except through the fact that the **finite** <term>original: *das Endliche*. The limited, material aspect of an object.</term> within it is absolutely joined to the **infinite** <term>original: *das Unendliche*. The eternal or boundless aspect.</term>. Once joined in such a manner, these can never and in no way be separated; and whatever we might distinguish within the **moved object** <term>original: *das Bewegte*. The celestial body in motion.</term> is never just the one or the other, but is always and necessarily the unity itself of the finite and the infinite.

None of the **spheres** <term>original: *Sphären*. Referring to the traditional cosmological idea of the transparent orbits or shells that carry the planets.</term> is therefore determined by anything other than its own innate excellence, which consists in the fact that it makes that by which it is set apart into absolute unity itself, and in turn, the unity itself into that by which it is set apart,

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of planetary motion. Having established on the previous page that orbits have two focal points (representing a tension between unity and individuality), the text now explains how the planets themselves "resolve" this tension through their variable speed, maintaining a higher philosophical "unity" despite their physical path being an ellipse rather than a perfect circle.</meta>

...even the **All** <note>The Universe or the Absolute Whole.</note>, and is absolute and independent, so that within the **difference** <term>original: *Differenz*. Here, the state of being a distinct, individual object rather than an undifferentiated part of the whole.</term> itself, the unity and the specific **destiny** <note>original: *Verhängniß*. Often translated as fate, but here referring to the essential nature or "calling" of a celestial body.</note> of each might be recognized: to be absolute as a particular being, and to be a particular thing while remaining absolute.

However, because difference should exist only as an appearance, while in truth or "in itself" there should be none, those **celestial creatures** <note>The planets, personified here as beings following a divine law.</note> have been taught by a truly divine art to moderate and restrain the course of their movements at times, and at others to follow their indwelling impulse more freely. In this way, time and space are once again made equal. Distance—which only remains "living" through its equality with the time inherent to the body—does not cease to be alive. Thus, at a greater distance, the body covers a smaller arc in the same amount of time that it covers a larger arc when the distance is smaller. <note>This is a philosophical interpretation of Kepler’s Second Law of Planetary Motion, which states that a line segment joining a planet and the Sun sweeps out equal areas during equal intervals of time.</note>

Through this more-than-mortal wisdom, which preserves equality within difference itself, it happens that the **celestial bodies** <note>original: *Gestirne*. Literally "stars," but used here for planets.</note>, whose orbits appear to be transcended circular lines, nevertheless describe truly **circular paths** in accordance with the "Idea." <note>The author argues that the ellipse is not a "failed" circle, but a "higher" circle that maintains perfect balance through the relationship between speed and distance.</note>

<summary>The author explains how planets maintain a philosophical "unity" by varying their speed based on their distance from the sun (Kepler's Second Law), arguing that this allows the elliptical orbit to fulfill the ideal of a perfect circle.</summary>

If you wish me to say, o friend, by what laws the order, number, size, and the other observable properties of the stars are determined, then I say that, as far as the order is concerned, the **matter** <term>original: *Materie*. <meta>This page completes the sentence from page 111, which contrasted two regions of the universe. It develops the idea that "Time" is not just a measurement, but a physical organizing force that creates axes and poles (North and South) within celestial bodies and the cosmos as a whole.</meta>

...ctly within themselves, and are less independent.

And since every thing to which **time** is vitally linked also bears an outward expression of this—which is the line and, in connection with **matter** <note>original: *Materie*. The physical substance of the universe.</note>, its coherence and solidity—so among those more perfect beings, first, the imprint of time—the line—is implanted in each individual, which we call its <term>axis<gloss>original: *Axe*. The central line around which a body rotates or is balanced.</gloss></term>, and whose extreme points are designated as **South** and **North**. 

Furthermore, this is also impressed upon the whole in such a way that all together form a collective line; depending on the position they occupy within it, they show a greater or lesser degree of coherence and internal unity, while the extreme points of the whole again relate to one another as **South** and **North**.

Those <term>spheres<gloss>original: *Sphären*. The whole, however, is so fashioned as an image of **God** that it possesses time absolutely within itself—being itself, therefore, in no time—and is a living creature so constituted that it cannot die.

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of existence, building on the distinction between the "primeval night" (the ground of existence) and the "light" (the ideal). Here, the author shifts focus from inanimate matter to the "organic being," exploring how life allows an individual thing to contain the potential for infinity.</meta>

Thus, only so much of the universe enters into the soul of each thing as the thing itself has represented of it. The purely physical thing, as we know, is necessarily and infinitely a singular individual.

The organic being, on the other hand—in which light and form become the substance itself—contains within its **concept** <note>original: *Begriff*. In this philosophical context, the "concept" is the internal essence or "blueprint" of a creature that dictates its development.</note> the possibility of infinitely many things beyond the individual. This might be the possibility of its own self in infinite generations through **reproduction**, or the possibility of other things that are distinct from it and which it connects to itself through **motion**. Or finally, it may be the possibility of other things that are simultaneously distinct from it and yet within it; this occurs because the **Idea** itself is embodied within the being, which, in relation to a **difference**, acts as the **perceiving subject** <note>original: *das Anschauende*. This refers to the capacity for "intuition" or conscious observation, where the mind takes in external objects.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 151 concerning how the finite relates to the infinite. The previous page discussed how the "reality" of individual existence is purely ideal; this page expands on that by arguing that neither soul nor body has independent "being" outside of their reciprocal relationship and their shared origin in the Absolute.</meta>

...is made capable, through its way of being in the **Absolute** <term>Absolute: the ultimate, unconditional reality that exists independently of any external relations or human perception</term>, of being a unity unto itself.

Thus, neither can a thing be defined by **duration** <note>original: *Dauer*. In this context, it refers to existence within time, as opposed to eternal existence.</note> except insofar as it is the object of a soul which is finite and whose existence is defined by duration; nor, conversely, can the existence of the soul be defined as duration except insofar as it is defined as the concept of a single existing thing. For this reason, the soul is no more something "in itself" <note>original: *an sich*. A key philosophical term referring to the true, objective nature of a thing regardless of how it is perceived.</note> than the body is; for both the former and the latter exist temporally only in relation to each other. "In itself," there is only the unity of both within that which is not subject to duration—within the **most blessed Nature** <note>original: *allerseligsten Natur*. A reference to the divine or the fundamental substance of the universe, where all contradictions are resolved.</note>, where possibility is not separated from reality, nor thought from **Being**. This is the **archetype** <note>original: *Urbild*. The original, perfect model of which all worldly things are mere copies.</note>, which is uncreated and truly imperishable.

For neither the soul, which relates directly to the body, is immortal—since the body is not, and the soul's existence is only determinable and defined by duration as long as the body lasts—nor is even the **soul of the soul** <note>original: *Seele der Seele*. This likely refers to a higher level of self-consciousness or the spiritual essence that reflects upon the basic animating soul.</note>, which relates to the soul just as the soul relates to the body.

<summary>The author argues that soul and body are not independent entities but exist temporally only through their relationship to each other, finding their true, eternal unity only within the "Absolute Nature." <meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation from the previous page, further detailing the relationship between the soul, the body, and the Absolute. While the previous page established that the soul and body only exist temporally in relation to each other, this page explores how the "finitude" of the soul prevents it from experiencing the perfect unity of possibility and reality that exists within the divine.</meta>

Furthermore, since the soul exists only through its relative **opposition** <note>original: *Gegensatz*. In this philosophical context, it refers to the way two things define each other by their differences.</note> to the body and is therefore not something that exists "in itself" <note>original: *an sich*. A central philosophical term referring to the true, objective nature of a thing independent of how it is perceived or its relations to other things.</note> at all, it appears only through this opposition. Consequently, it is destined for **existence** <term>existence: or *Daseyn*, referring to a specific, limited state of being in the world, as opposed to eternal or absolute Being</term> only insofar as it is the **concept** <note>original: *Begriff*. In German idealism, a "concept" is often seen as the internal logic or blueprint of a thing's existence.</note> of an individual being. 

This existence, however, is not determined by a mere link with a physical thing, but rather through the soul’s own **finitude** <note>original: *Endlichkeit*. The quality of being limited in time, space, or power.</note>. It is by virtue of this finitude that the union of **possibility** and **reality**—which are perfectly joined in God—remains outside the soul’s own grasp. In God, what *can* be (possibility) and what *is* (reality) are one and the same; however, for the finite soul, these two states are separated. 

For those concepts which are the immediate concepts of finite things behave exactly like the things themselves: they are opposed to the infinite concept just as finite things are. They only align with the infinite concept insofar as they are "infinite within their finitude."

Just as a physical thing establishes its own sense of time by containing a reality whose possibility (or a possibility whose reality) lies outside of itself, so too does the concept, insofar as it is purely finite. And just as it is with physical things, so it is with the immediate concepts of things: there exists that infinite unity in which every possibility [meets] its real— <note>The text breaks off here mid-word: *Wirk—* (likely *Wirklichkeit* or "reality/actuality").</note>

...through its manner of being in the **Absolute** <note>original: *Absoluten*. In this philosophical tradition, the "Absolute" refers to the ultimate, unconditioned reality or God, in which all contradictions are resolved.</note>, it is made capable of being its own **unity** <note>original: *Einheit*. The state of being a singular, coherent whole.</note>.

This is the **archetype** <term>archetype: or *Urbild*, the uncreated, eternal original pattern or ideal form of a thing</term>, which is uncreated and truly imperishable.

This existence, however, is not determined by a mere connection with a physical thing, but rather by the soul’s own **finitude** <note>original: *Endlichkeit*. The state of being limited, particularly by time.</note>. 

It is by virtue of this finitude that **possibility**—which in **God** is joined to its **actuality**—and conversely **actuality**—which in Him is joined to its **possibility**—lie outside of the soul itself. <note>The author argues that for God, to think of something is for it to be real (possibility and actuality are one). For finite beings like us, there is a gap: we can imagine things that aren't real, and real things often fall short of their potential.</note> For the concepts that are the immediate concepts of finite things behave just like the things themselves; they stand in opposition to the **infinite concept** <note>The divine mind or the Absolute.</note> in the same way those things do, and they only correspond to it insofar as they are infinite within their own finitude.

Just as a physical thing establishes its own sense of time by containing an **actuality** from which its **possibility** is separated (or a possibility from which its actuality is separated), the same is true for the **concept**, provided it is purely finite. And just as it is regarding physical things, so it is also regarding the immediate concepts of things: that infinite unity, in which every possibility [meets] its actu[ality]... <note>The text ends mid-word: *Wirklichkeit* (actuality).</note>

<summary>The author explains that the soul’s temporal existence arises from its finitude, characterized by the separation of possibility and actuality. Unlike the divine mind where thought and reality are one, the finite soul experiences these as distinct, creating the conditions for temporal life.</summary>
<keywords>soul and body, finitude, actuality and possibility, Being, Daseyn, God, Concept, German Idealism, temporal existence</keywords>

Referring to the realm of thought, mind, or spiritual essence.</note>. Therefore, the difference between the Ideal and the Real is itself a difference within the sphere of concepts.

But to completely untangle this being, which is entwined from the finite and the infinite, is only possible for one who perceives that—and how—everything is contained in everything, and that the fullness of the whole is deposited even within the individual.

Just as it has now become clear to us that the **Infinite**, the **Finite**, and the **Eternal**, when subordinated to the finite or to **difference**, appear as **space**, and when subordinated to the infinite or to **relative unity**, appear as **time**, so it is evident that this same unity, viewed under the form of the **Eternal**, is **Reason** itself, and expresses itself as Reason within the **concept**.

<meta>This page continues the transition from the discussion of Reason and its expression as a "Concept" to its structural organization in formal logic—specifically how arithmetic, geometry, and philosophy relate to the logical structures of judgment and the syllogism.</meta>

From which the unity and the diversity of the three sciences—**Arithmetic**, **Geometry**, and **Philosophy**—might also be easily understood.

To further develop the **organism** of **reflected Reason** in **Judgment** <note>original: *Urtheil*. In classical logic, a judgment is the mental act of connecting two concepts via a copula (e.g., "The rose is red").</note>—which, aside from the previously defined difference between judgment and the **Concept**, is the same as it is in the concept—would be unnecessary work.

Concerning the **Syllogism** <term>Syllogism: *Schluß*. A form of logical reasoning where a conclusion is drawn from two premises (e.g., All men are mortal; Socrates is a man; therefore, Socrates is mortal).</term>, however, which posits the **Eternal** as infinite, it is enough to note the following: since **Possibility**, **Reality**, and **Necessity** are already present together within each, all further variation regarding the syllogism is limited to the fact that the unity of these three (which are present in all syllogisms) is expressed either under the form of the **Infinite**, the **Finite**, or the **Eternal**.

The infinite form is the **categorical**, the finite is the **hypothetical**, and the one that partakes most in the nature of the Eternal is the **disjunctive** <note>These represent the three "Relations" of judgments in Kantian logic: Categorical (asserting a fact), Hypothetical (an 'if-then' dependency), and Disjunctive (an 'either-or' relationship).</note>. In every syllogism, however, despite all their differences, the **major premise** <note>original: *Obersaz*. The first proposition in a syllogism, typically containing the broader general truth.</note> in relation to the **minor premise** <note>original: *Untersaz*. The second proposition, which provides the specific case.</note> is always categorical or infinite, while the minor premise is hypothetical and fin- <note>The text cuts off here, likely intended to be "finite" (*endlich*).</note>

<summary>This page explores how the "Eternal" is structured within logic, specifically through the three forms of the syllogism: categorical, hypothetical, and disjunctive. It suggests that these logical structures provide the foundation for understanding the relationship between mathematics and philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Arithmetic, Geometry, Philosophy, Reason, Judgment, Syllogism, Categorical, Hypothetical, Disjunctive, Major Premise, Minor Premise</keywords>

...ly, but the **conclusion** <note>original: *Schlußsatz*. The final statement in a formal logical argument.</note> is disjunctive and unites both the former and the latter within itself.

**Lucian.** O admirable form of the **Understanding**! <note>original: *Verstand*. In the philosophy of this period, "Understanding" refers to the human capacity to organize sensory data and logic, whereas "Reason" (*Vernunft*) is often seen as a higher faculty that perceives the absolute.</note> What a joy it is to fathom your relations, and to recognize the same imprint of the **Eternal**, from the scaffolding of physical things up to the form of the syllogism. <meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of the Absolute. It completes the sentence from the previous page, arguing that in the highest state of reality, the act of "knowing" and the state of "being" are not two different things, but a single, unified essence.</meta>

—lute <note>original: *luter*—the conclusion of the word *absoluter* (absolute) from the previous page.</note> **Knowing**, according to its form, it is also expressed in it according to its essence. Therefore, with regard to the **Absolute**, no **Being** <note>original: *Seyn*</note> remains behind that could be placed in opposition to a **Knowing** <note>original: *Erkennen*</note>. Yet, if we conversely wished to define the **Absolute** as absolute knowing, we could again not do so in the sense that we opposed this absolute knowing to **Being**; for, when viewed absolutely, true **Being** exists only in the **Idea**, and yet the **Idea** is also the **Substance** and **Being** itself.

However, as the <term>Indifference|Indifferenz: A central concept in German Idealism referring to a point of "neutrality" where subject and object, or thought and matter, are one and the same.</term> of **Knowing** and **Being**, the **Absolute** can once more only be defined in relation to **Reason** <note>original: *Vernunft*</note>, because it is only within Reason that **Knowing** and **Being** can appear as opposites.

Yet, he who would wish to define the nature of the **Absolute** through the concept of **Activity** <note>original: *Thätigkeit*—referring to the act of doing, striving, or the exercise of will.</note>, so as not to define it as **Being**, would move the furthest away from the **Idea** of the **Absolute**.

For all opposition between **Activity** and **Being** belongs only to the <term>reflected world|abgebildete Welt: The world of everyday appearances and physical objects, which the author views as a mere shadow or "image" of the higher, absolute reality.</term>, insofar as—apart from that which is simply and in...

<summary>The author argues that in the Absolute, there is no distinction between existence and thought, as both are unified in the "Idea." He warns that trying to describe the Absolute as "Activity" is a mistake, as such distinctions only exist in the world of appearances, not in the ultimate reality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue initiated by Anselmo on the previous page. ...various expressions of reason as it takes shape in philosophy.

**Alexander.** Regarding the fates of that doctrine, my friends, which takes its name from matter, I can briefly show that its experiences have been no different than those which every other speculative doctrine <note>original: *spekulative Lehre*; refers to theoretical or philosophical systems that seek to understand the fundamental nature of reality through reason.</note> has likewise undergone in the course of time; and that this doctrine, too, found its own demise only in the demise of philosophy itself. For what has been handed down to us by the ancients concerning the meaning of that doctrine is sufficient to teach us that it carried within itself the seeds of the highest speculation, developed to a greater or lesser degree.

The true idea of matter, however, was lost early on, and has been known to only a few at any given time.

It is the unity of the divine and natural principle <term>Principle: from the Latin *principium*, the fundamental source or origin from which everything else arises.</term> itself—absolutely simple, therefore, immutable, and eternal.

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue between Bruno and Lucian. The previous page ended mid-sentence, with the speakers preparing to examine the conflict between Realism and Idealism. This page begins by establishing that both systems must share the same ultimate goal—the Absolute—and seeks to define where their differences actually lie.</meta>

...take as our foundation: to which **Realism** must **Idealism** be opposed, and to which **Idealism** must **Realism** be opposed?

**Lucian.** Above all, it therefore seems necessary to state generally how **Idealism** and **Realism** can be distinguished from one another. It cannot be by their object, if both aim for the highest **mode of knowledge** <term>Mode of Knowledge (Erkenntnisart): The specific way in which the mind perceives or understands truth, ranging from simple sensory perception to the highest form of philosophical reasoning.</term>, for this object is necessarily only **One**. If, however, they are not of a **speculative nature** <term>Speculative (Spekulation): In this context, it refers to deep philosophical thinking that seeks to understand the fundamental nature of reality beyond mere physical observation.</term> at all—either one of them or both—then in the first case no comparison is possible, and in the other, it is not worth the effort to investigate their difference. The "One" of all **philosophy**, however, is the **Absolute** <note>original: "das Absolute" — In German Idealism, the Absolute refers to the ultimate, unconditioned reality that underlies all existence and transcends all contradictions between mind and matter.</note>.

**Bruno.** This, therefore, must be the object of the highest mode of knowledge in both in the same way.

**Lucian.** Necessarily.

**Bruno.** Do you mean, then, that they differ through the **mode of contemplation**?

**Lucian.** I think so.

**Bruno.** But how? Is there in the **Absolute** a difference or duality,

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue between Bruno and Lucian regarding the nature of the Absolute and the origins of philosophical systems. On the previous page, Bruno asked if the Absolute contains a duality or if it is a single unity. This page begins with the conclusion of that question and moves toward defining the specific terms "Real" and "Ideal" as they relate to "Essence" and "Form."</meta>

or is it not rather necessarily and simply **One**?

*Lucian.* Not a duality within itself, but only in the **contemplation** <term>Contemplation (Betrachtung): The act of observing or thinking about a subject from a specific philosophical vantage point; the "lens" through which one views reality.</term>. For while the **Real** in it is contemplated, **Realism** arises; while the **Ideal** is contemplated, **Idealism**. In itself, however, the **Real** is also the **Ideal**, and conversely, the **Ideal** is the **Real**.

*Bruno.* It seems necessary that you define what you call the **Real** and what you call the **Ideal**; for these words, as we know, are subject to very different meanings.

*Lucian.* Under the **Real**, then, let us understand in this investigation generally the **Essence** <term>Essence (Wesen): The fundamental nature or "what-ness" of a thing; its internal being regardless of its outward appearance.</term>, and under the **Ideal**, the **Form**.

*Bruno.* **Realism** would therefore arise through reflection on the **Essence**, while **Idealism** arises by holding fast to the **Form** of the **Absolute**.

*Lucian.* That is so.

*Bruno.* But how—did we not say that in the **Absolute**, **Form** and **Essence** are necessarily **One**?

<summary>In this passage, Lucian and Bruno define the philosophical divide between Realism and Idealism as a matter of perspective rather than a split in reality itself. They equate the "Real" with "Essence" and the "Ideal" with "Form," setting the stage for a deeper investigation into how these two aspects unify within the Absolute.</summary>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Lucian and Bruno. On the previous page, they established that Realism (Essence) and Idealism (Form) are two ways of looking at the same Absolute. This page explores the term "Indifference" as the point where these two perspectives become one.</meta>

*Lucian.* As necessarily as **essence** is distinguished from **form** in the finite <term>The Finite (das Endliche): Refers to the physical world of limited things, such as objects or individuals, which have boundaries in time and space, as opposed to the infinite Absolute.</term>.

*Bruno.* But how are they **one**?

*Lucian.* Not through a mere connection, but in such a way that each is the same for itself; each, namely, is for itself the whole **Absolute**.

*Bruno.* **Realism**, then, and **Idealism**—with the one viewing the Absolute according to its essence, and the other according to its form—viewed only **one** thing in both (if it can be called a "thing" at all), necessarily and without contradiction—one single object.

*Lucian.* Evidently.

*Bruno.* But how would one best designate such a unity, which rests not on a simple coexistence, but on a total identity?

*Lucian.* We have already, if I recall, not improperly designated it as **Indifference** <term>Indifference (Indifferenz): A technical philosophical term referring to a state where two opposing things—like mind and matter—are identical and indistinguishable within the Absolute.</term>, and thereby expressed that very neutrality of value <note>original: "Gleichgültigkeit." While often translated as "indifference" or "apathy" in modern German, in this philosophical context it literally means "equal value" (gleich-gültig), meaning both sides are equally valid or identical.</note> for our contemplation.

*Bruno.* If, however, **Idealism** and **Realism** are the highest oppositions of philosophy, does not the very insight into philosophy itself rest upon the insight into this **Indifference**—

<summary>Lucian and Bruno conclude that Realism and Idealism are simply different perspectives on the same Absolute. They introduce the concept of "Indifference" to describe the state where these two perspectives are perfectly identical and indistinguishable.</summary>
<keywords>Indifference, Absolute, Essence, Form, Idealism, Realism, Identity, Contemplation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Lucian and Bruno. The previous page ended mid-sentence with Bruno asking if philosophy rests upon the insight into "Indifference"—the point where all opposites vanish.</meta>

—without any opposition, of philosophy as such?

*Bruno.* Let us search further into this highest of all mysteries. *Lucian.* Certainly.

*Bruno.* Regarding **form**, however, we agreed that it is the form of both the one and the other—namely, of **ideality** and **reality**, of the **subjective** and the **objective**—but both existing with equal infinity.

*Lucian.* That follows naturally.

*Bruno.* A cognition, therefore, that is equally and infinitely ideal and real, is an **absolute cognition**.

*Lucian.* Quite certainly.

*Bruno.* An absolute cognition, furthermore—

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Bruno and Lucian regarding the nature of absolute cognition. The previous page concluded that when the union of the subjective and objective is viewed as an active process, it constitutes "absolute cognition."</meta>

...is not **thought** in opposition to a **being**; rather, it already holds thought and being themselves united within it, and in an absolute manner.

*Lucian.* Indisputably.

*Bruno.* Therefore, it also holds thought and being *under* it, not over it.

*Lucian.* It is necessarily higher than both of these, insofar as they are opposites.

*Bruno.* This **cognition** <term>Cognition (Erkennen): In this context, it refers to a higher, divine form of knowing where the "thinker" and the "thing thought about" are one and the same.</term>, however, stands in a relationship of **absolute indifference** <term>Absolute Indifference (absolute Indifferenz): A core concept in this philosophy describing a point of perfect balance or identity where all distinctions—such as those between mind and matter—cease to exist.</term> to the essence of the Eternal.

*Lucian.* Necessarily so, since it is the form.

*Bruno.* But since it holds thought and being under it, we will find it impossible to make thought or being into immediate **attributes** <term>Attributes (Attribute): Fundamental characteristics or qualities of a substance. *Lucian.* Impossible.

*Lucian.* Nevermore shall we be able to do this.

<summary>Bruno and Lucian argue that since the Absolute is the source of both thought and being, these two categories cannot be considered its immediate attributes. *Bruno.* But those who, in whatever way it may be, make **thought** as such the **principle** <term>Principle (Princip): A fundamental source or basis from which everything else is derived.</term> and absolutely oppose **being** to it, we shall count entirely among the **immature** <note>original: "Unmündigen" – This term literally refers to minors or those not yet of legal age; here, Bruno uses it to describe those who have not yet reached philosophical maturity.</note> in philosophy.

That from which something is separated,

<summary>Bruno argues that true philosophical maturity requires seeing thought and being as identical within absolute cognition, rather than opposing them. <meta>This page continues the dialogue between Bruno and Lucian. Bruno is concluding his point that absolute cognition is inherently simple and only appears divided into thought and being when viewed through the lens of finite existence.</meta>

...does not need to contain what is separated, but can be simply simple. ...ity <note>original: "lichkeit"; completing a word like "Wirklichkeit" (reality) or "Einheit" (unity) from the previous page.</note> with possibility in time is never possible; it is not absolutely posited <note>To "posit" something (setzen) is to assume its existence or truth as a fundamental starting point for an argument.</note>, but absolutely demanded. **Bruno.** Will not a philosophy founded upon such knowledge be full—

# N o t e s .

---

**On the divine and natural principle of things.)** For a preliminary explanation, see the passage in Plato <note>original: "Plato (in Tim. p. 385.)"</note>: We must distinguish two kinds of causes: the necessary and the divine. We should seek the divine in all things for the sake of achieving a happy life, insofar as our nature allows. <note>original Greek: "Δυο αιτιας είδη χρη διοριζεσθαι, το μεν αναγκαιον το δε θειον, και το μεν θειον εν άπασι ζητειν κτησεως ένεκα ευδαιμονος βιου καθ’ όσον ἡμων ἡ φυσις ενδεχεται." This quote is from Plato's *Timaeus*, a foundational text on the origin and nature of the universe.</note>

**P. 32. so that Sophocles etc.)** In a fragment preserved by Plutarch, <note>Plutarch (c. 46–120 AD) was a Greek biographer and essayist whose works often preserved fragments of lost classical literature.</note> which can be found in the *Collected Works of Sophocles*, edited by Brunck, Volume IV, p. 686.

# Notes.

**On the divine and natural principle of things.)** For a preliminary explanation, let the passage from Plato serve <note>original: "Plato (in Tim. p. 385.)"</note>: Two kinds of causes must be distinguished: the necessary and the divine. We should seek the divine in all things for the sake of attaining a happy life, insofar as our nature allows. <note>original Greek: "Δυο αιτιας ειδη χρη διοριζεσθαι, το μεν αναγκαιον το δε θειον, και το μεν θειον εν άπασι ζητειν κτησεως ενεκα ευδαιμονος βιου καθ’ όσον ἡμων ἡ φυσις ενδεχεται." This is a famous passage from the *Timaeus*, where Plato explores the origins of the universe.</note>

**P. 32. so that Sophocles etc.)** In a fragment which Plutarch <note>Plutarch was a Greek philosopher and biographer whose works often preserved snippets of earlier literature that would otherwise be lost.</note> has preserved, and which is found in the *Complete Works of Sophocles*, edited by Brunck, Vol. IV, p. 686. <note>Richard François Philippe Brunck (1729–1803) was a renowned French classical scholar known for his editions of Greek poets.</note>

**P. 52. Socrates in Plato etc.)** In the passage of the *Philebus*, p. 217. <note>The *Philebus* is a late Platonic dialogue concerning the nature of pleasure and its relation to the good life.</note>

**P. 106. Note then, O friend, the meaning of the laws which a divine intellect seems to have revealed to us.)** These are understood to be Kepler's laws. <note>Johannes Kepler (1571–1630) formulated three laws of planetary motion. The author here treats them not just as physics, but as "revealed" divine truths.</note> To sense their speculative <note>In this context, "speculative" refers to deep philosophical or metaphysical contemplation rather than mere guessing.</note> meaning, one must first have freed them from later empirical and mechanical distortions and recognized them in their purity. Concerning this, one can—

<meta>This page continues the scholarly annotations from the previous page, specifically concluding a discussion on Kepler’s laws before transitioning into Section 186, which identifies the historical sources for the "Bruno" character and his philosophical doctrines.</meta>

...we can confidently refer to the earlier efforts of a friend: the positive aspect of the view of these laws expressed here is consistent with the general scheme of construction prevailing in this dialogue. For according to this scheme, the three Keplerian laws <note>Johannes Kepler (1571–1630) formulated three laws of planetary motion. Here, they are interpreted through a philosophical triad: Indifference (unity), Difference (multiplicity), and Totality (synthesis).</note> relate to one another generally as Indifference, Difference, and that in which both are reconstructed into a unity: Totality. In this way, they perfectly express the entire organism of reason and form a self-contained system. This may serve as a preliminary guiding star for understanding Bruno’s speeches for those who do not wish to wait for further explanations to be provided elsewhere.

§. 186. The **fates**, then, of that **doctrine** **etc.**) For those familiar with the subject, it perhaps requires no reminder that the following presentation most closely approaches the particular way in which **Giordano Bruno** <note>original: "Jordanus Brunus"</note> presented the doctrine of the universe. This is especially true of the brilliant excerpt from his work **On Cause, Principle, and Unity** <note>original: "Von der Ursache, dem Princip und dem Einen." This refers to Bruno's 1584 Italian dialogue *De la causa, principio et uno*.</note> given as an appendix to the letters on the doctrine of Spinoza by Jacobi. <note>Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi (1743–1819) was a key figure in the "Pantheism Controversy." His summary of Bruno’s work helped revive interest in the Renaissance philosopher during the late 18th century.</note>

Except that Bruno declares the **soul** and the **form** of a thing to be identical (loc. cit. p. 269) <note>original: "a. a. O." (am angeführten Ort), meaning "in the place cited."</note>, which makes it impossible for him to achieve the highest point of indifference between **matter** and **form** with consistent clarity; **Alexander**, on the other hand, [sees] the soul itself as

<summary>The author explains how Kepler’s laws form a "system of reason" and identifies Giordano Bruno’s "On Cause, Principle, and Unity" as a primary source for the text's philosophy.</summary>
