--- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly annotations from page 226. The previous page established that the dialogue's philosophical structure reflects Giordano Bruno’s doctrine of the universe, specifically referencing the summary provided by Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi. This page provides direct quotes from Bruno to support the claim that "form" and "matter" are ultimately unified in a first principle.</meta>

...claims the one opposition in form, and subsequently subordinates it to form, the following passages from Bruno <note>original: "Brunus." Giordano Bruno (1548–1600) was an Italian philosopher and cosmological theorist who proposed that the stars were distant suns surrounded by their own planets. He was eventually executed by the Inquisition.</note> may be regarded as evidence and parallels for his presentation.

> “We must distinguish from accidental form the necessary, eternal, and first form, which is the form and source of all forms.”

> “This first universal form and that first universal matter <note>In Bruno’s philosophy, matter is not just physical "stuff" but a divine principle that possesses the potential for all things.</note>—how are they united, inseparable, distinct, and yet only one Being? We must seek to resolve this riddle.” pp. 282, 283.

> “The perfect possibility of the existence <note>original: "Daseyn"</note> of things cannot precede their actual existence, nor can it remain after it. If there were a perfect possibility of actually being without actual existence, things would create themselves and exist before they were. The first and most perfect principle <note>original: "Princip"</note> encompasses all existence within itself; it can be everything, and is everything. Active force and potency <term>potency: translated from the German "Potenz," referring to the latent power or capacity of a thing to become something else</term>, possibility and reality, are therefore an undivided and inseparable One within it. It is not so with other things, which can be or not be, or can be determined this way or that. Every human being is, at every moment, what they are in that moment; but they are not everything that they can be in general and according to substance <note>The author uses "Substanz" to refer to the underlying essence of a thing that remains unchanged throughout different states of being.</note>. That which is all it can be is only a Single Being, which in its existence encompasses all other existence.” p. 284.

<summary>This page provides key excerpts from Giordano Bruno's philosophy to illustrate the identity of form and matter in the "First Principle." It argues that in the ultimate reality, the capacity to be (potency) and actual existence are one and the same.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, form and matter, existence, first principle, active force, potency, possibility and reality, substance, Friedrich Heinrich Jacobi</keywords>

--- Page 250 ---
<meta>This page continues the series of quotations from Giordano Bruno, providing further evidence for the unity of form and matter. It builds on the previous page's discussion of the "first principle" where possibility and reality coincide.</meta>

> “The universe, the uncreated Nature, is likewise everything it can be in actuality and all at once, because it contains within itself all matter along with the eternal, unchangeable form of its shifting shapes. However, in its developments from moment to moment—its particular parts, qualities, individual beings, and its externality in general—it is no longer what it truly is and can be. In those aspects, it is merely a shadow of the image of the first principle, in which active force and potency <term>potency: the latent capacity or power for something to happen or exist, as opposed to its actual realization</term>, possibility and reality, are one and the same.” pp. 285 f.

> “We have no eye for either the height of this light or the depth of this abyss; concerning which the holy books, by joining both extreme ends together, say with sublimity: Darkness shall not be darkened by you. Night shall be enlightened like the day. As its darkness is, so also is its light. <note>original: "Tenebrae non obscurabuntur a te. Nox sicut Dies illuminabitur. Sicut tenebrae ejus ita et lumen ejus." These verses are from Psalm 139:12, used here by Bruno to illustrate the coincidence of opposites in the divine principle.</note>” p. 287.

> “One must beware of mixing the matter of the second kind <note>Bruno distinguishes between "physical matter," which changes and decays, and "prime matter," which is a metaphysical foundation for all things, both spiritual and physical.</note>, which is the subject only of natural and changeable things, with that matter which the sensible and supersensible worlds have in common.” p. 287.

> “This matter, which lies at the foundation of incorporeal as well as corporeal things, is a manifold being insofar as it encompasses a multitude of forms within itself; yet, considered in itself, it is absolutely simple and indivisible. Because it is everything, it can be nothing in particular. I confess that it is not for everyone <note>The text breaks off here, suggesting the following page will complete the thought on the difficulty of grasping the nature of prime matter.</note>

<summary>This page presents excerpts from Giordano Bruno regarding the nature of the universe and matter, arguing that in the "first principle," all possibilities are already actualized. It emphasizes the distinction between common physical matter and a higher, metaphysical matter that underlies both the physical and spiritual realms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Giordano Bruno’s philosophy, specifically focusing on the paradox of the Absolute—how it can be everything and nothing simultaneously. It transitions into a discussion of how these ideas relate to the later philosophy of Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, as voiced by the character Anselmo in this dialogue.</meta>

it is easy to grasp how something can possess all properties and yet none; how it can be the formal essence of everything and yet have no form of its own; nevertheless, the philosopher is familiar with the proposition: *the same thing cannot be the whole and also a specific thing* <note>original: "non potest esse idem, totum et aliquid." This suggests that the Absolute (the "whole") cannot be identified with any single, finite object ("a something").</note> p. 290, p. 197.

Not without reason, o friend, etc.) <note>The text here refers to the dialogue's interlocutor, Anselmo.</note> Anselmo, while on the one hand aligning himself with Leibnizian intellectualism <term>Intellectualism: the philosophical view that reality is best understood through reason and the intellect rather than through the senses alone</term>, seems on the other hand to be constrained in his presentation by its primary limitation, which lies in starting from the concept of the monad <term>monad: for Leibniz, these are the basic, indivisible units of reality—the "spiritual atoms" that make up the universe</term>; at the same time, however, the question might arise whether that doctrine has not actually been reinterpreted here in a higher sense, and whether, amidst the complications and the lack of form necessitated by that initial limitation, an expression of true philosophy has ever broken through with such clarity as several points in Anselmo’s speech—for instance, the idea that only inadequate representation <term>inadequate representation: a partial or distorted way of seeing the world where things seem separate from their source</term> makes things appear to be outside of God, and the like. This question is all the more natural because, up to our own time, even by those who profess to follow Leibniz or wish to return philosophy to him, his doctrine has remained entirely misunderstood in its main points—not without his own fault—concerning issues such as pre-established harmony <term>pre-established harmony: the theory that the soul and body do not influence each other directly, but were synchronized by God from the beginning, like two perfectly timed clocks</term> (which is applied to the connection of the body with the soul), the relationship of monads to God, and so forth; nevertheless, one might find nothing in Anselmo’s speech that could not actually be supported by individual passages from Leibniz...

<meta>This page continues the analysis of Giordano Bruno’s philosophical dialogues, specifically contrasting the concept of the Absolute with the character Anselmo’s perspective. The previous page discussed the nature of the Universe as encompassing all possibilities; this page examines the difficulty of conceptualizing a being that is both everything and nothing, while bridging these ideas with the philosophy of Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz.</meta>

...easy to grasp how something can possess all properties and yet none; how it can be the formal essence of everything and yet have no form of its own; nevertheless, the philosopher <term>philosopher: original "Weltweisen," literally "world-wise one"</term> is familiar with the maxim: **the same thing cannot be the whole and a specific thing at once,** <note>original Latin: "non potest esse idem, totum et aliquid." A logical principle suggesting that the universal whole is distinct from any of its individual, finite parts.</note> p. 290,

(P. 197. **Not without reason, oh friend,** etc.) Anselmo, while on one hand aligning himself with Leibnizian intellectualism <term>intellectualism: a philosophical approach emphasizing the mind or intellect as the primary source of knowledge and reality</term>, seems on the other hand restricted by its inherent limitation, which lies in starting from the concept of the Monad <term>Monad: in Leibniz's philosophy, an indivisible, simple substance that reflects the entire universe from its own perspective</term>; his presentation appears similarly confined. At the same time, however, the question could arise whether that doctrine has not actually been reinterpreted here in a higher sense, and whether, amidst the complications and the lack of form necessitated by that first restriction, any expression of true philosophy has ever broken through with such clarity as seen in several points of Anselmo's speech—for instance, the idea that **only an inadequate representation** <term>inadequate representation: a partial or confused perception that fails to see the underlying unity of all things in the divine</term> **allows things to be seen as existing outside of God**, and the like. This question is all the more natural because, up to our own time, even among those who claim to follow Leibniz or wish to trace philosophy back to him, his doctrine has remained completely misunderstood in its main points—not without some fault of his own. This includes concepts such as **pre-established harmony** <term>pre-established harmony: the theory that God coordinated the universe so that mind and body appear to interact despite being independent</term> (which is usually applied only to the connection between body and soul), the relationship of the Monads to God, and so forth. Nevertheless, one would likely find nothing in Anselmo’s speech that could not be supported by specific passages from Leibniz...

--- Page 254 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Anselmo's speech, specifically examining how his ideas might be understood through Leibnizian philosophy without merely relying on the general "spirit" of that system. It connects these ideas to the works of Nicolas Malebranche and Giordano Bruno.</meta>

...could be accomplished without the need to take refuge in an appeal to the spirit of the Intellectual System <term>Intellectual System: A reference to the philosophical framework of Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz and Christian Wolff, which emphasized rationalism and the order of the mind</term>. Concerning, for instance, the existence of unities in God, and the principle that for an adequate or rational representation, all things exist within God, one can refer to several remarks found partly in the *New Essays* <note>original French: *Nouveaux essais*, referring to Leibniz's *New Essays on Human Understanding*</note> itself, and partly in a supplement to them regarding the theorem of *Malebranche* <note>Nicolas Malebranche (1638–1715), a French priest and philosopher who famously argued that we "see all things in God"</note>—namely, that we see all things in God.

P. 221. **which a philosopher before us has left behind in these words, etc.**) This philosopher is likewise *Giordano Bruno*, whose words cited here (from the previously mentioned excerpt on p. 303) may indeed be regarded as the <term>credo: original Latin "Symbolum," meaning a summary of faith or a fundamental guiding principle</term> of true philosophy.
